<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We disprove the Gopalan–Servedio square-root conjecture, even up to an arbitrary constant factor. For every real <i>C</i> &gt; 0, there is a nonconstant Boolean function $`f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\{-1,1\}`$ on a finite sign cube such that
> 
> $`\displaystyle \sum_{i=1}^n \widehat f(\{i\})\gt C\sqrt{\deg(f)}.`$
> 
> Here $`\widehat f(\{i\})`$ is the linear Fourier coefficient associated with the <i>i</i>th input, and $`\deg(f)`$ is the degree of the real multilinear polynomial representing <i>f</i>.

- **claim-unbounded-singleton-degree-ratio** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every real $C>0$, there are a positive integer $n$ and a nonconstant Boolean function $f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\{-1,1\}$ such that $$\sum_{i=1}^n\widehat f(\{i\})>C\sqrt{\deg(f)}.$$ Consequently, $$\sup_{\substack{n\ge1,\ f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\{-1,1\}\\\deg(f)>0}} \frac{\sum_i\|\widehat f(\{i\})\|}{\sqrt{\deg(f)}}=\infty.$$
  - Explanation: The signed singleton sum can exceed any prescribed constant multiple of the square root of real multilinear degree. The corresponding absolute singleton ratio is unbounded.
- **claim-quadratic-predictor-transfer** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-transfer)): Let $Z_j$ be centered, variance-one random variables converging in distribution to a standard normal, with $\sup_j\mathbb EZ_j^4<\infty$. For fixed $r$, let $\mathbf Z_j$ consist of $r$ independent copies of $Z_j$, and let $\mathbf G$ consist of $r$ independent standard normals. If $g:\mathbb R^r\to\mathbb R$ is measurable, its discontinuities have Gaussian measure zero, and $$\|g(z)\|\le K\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^r z_i^2\right)$$ for some fixed $K$, then $\mathbb Eg(\mathbf Z_j)\to\mathbb Eg(\mathbf G)$.
  - Explanation: Joint weak convergence transfers expectations of a fixed predictor-error function when its discontinuities are Gaussian-null and its growth is quadratic. The uniform fourth-moment hypothesis controls clipping errors.
- **claim-boolean-score-readout** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-readout)): Suppose finite observations have positive cell degree bounds $D$ and arbitrarily large ratios $v(F)/D$. Then Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> holds.
  - Explanation: Arbitrarily large retained variance relative to a cell degree bound suffices for the Boolean counterexamples. Independent averaging makes the score suitable for a sign readout.
- **claim-fixed-cost-amplification** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-amplification)): Let $1\le d<r$ be integers, and let $\mathcal W$ be a measurable observation rule on $r$ real inputs. Suppose:* 1. *On any product of finite input alphabets, the indicator of each output value is a finite linear combination of functions each depending on at most $d$ input coordinates.* 2. *There is an $\eta>0$ such that, whenever centered variance-one laws $Z_j$ converge weakly to a standard normal with uniformly bounded fourth moments, independent copies satisfy, for all sufficiently large $j$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\!\left(\mathbb E\!\left[\sum_{i=1}^r Z_{j,i}\,\middle\|\, \mathcal W(\mathbf Z_j)\right]\right)\ge d+\eta.$$* *Then finite observations have arbitrarily large retained variance-to-cell-degree ratios, and Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> follows.
  - Explanation: A pointwise coordinate-cost saving together with a persistent variance advantage gives a fixed multiplicative improvement. Repeating finitely many stages makes the retained variance-to-degree ratio arbitrarily large.
- **claim-refined-report-cost-and-gain** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-report)): For every product of finite input alphabets, the rule $\mathcal W$ has finite range, and each output indicator is a finite linear combination of functions of at most $m$ input coordinates.* *For independent centered variance-one inputs, whose marginal laws need not agree, and every fixed $b\in\mathbb R$, put $$\Delta_b=\mathbb E[\mathbf1_A(1-(L-b)^2)].$$ Then $$\label{eq:retained} \operatorname{Var}(\mathbb E[L\mid\mathcal W])\ge m+\Delta_b.$$
  - Explanation: Cancellation gives the exceptional tag the same coordinate cost as the ordinary reports. A fixed predictor on that tag yields a lower bound for retained variance.
- **claim-gaussian-exceptional-event** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-gaussian)): There are a finite odd integer $m\ge3$, closed intervals $I_1,\ldots,I_m$, and a selector $\iota$ with finitely many cut points such that the following holds for independent standard normal inputs. Let $\beta_j$ be the probability of $I_j$, and let $\mu_j,s_j^2$ be the conditional mean and variance on $I_j$. Define $$p_* =\prod_{j=1}^m\beta_j,\qquad S=\sum_{j=1}^m s_j^2,\qquad b=\sum_{j=1}^m\mu_j-1.$$ Then $p_*>0$, $S<1/4$, and $$\label{eq:deficit} d_A:=\mathbb E[\mathbf1_A(1-(L-b)^2)]>\frac34p_*.$$ The boundary of $A$ lies in a finite union of coordinate hyperplanes. In particular, $\mathbb P(A)>0$ and $\operatorname{Var}(L\mid A)<1$.
  - Explanation: A sufficiently fine shifted interval grid and a tail fallback selector produce an exceptional event with positive probability and a strict prediction-error saving. The parameters are fixed at a finite stage.
- **claim-coarse-report-cost-and-error** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-coarse-report)): On every product of finite input alphabets, $\mathcal O$ has finite range, and each output indicator is a finite linear combination of functions of at most $m$ input coordinates.* *Let the inputs be independent, centered, and of variance one; their marginal laws need not agree. For any fixed constants $b_0,b_1\in\mathbb R$, define $$\label{eq:coarse-deficit} g_{b_0,b_1} =\mathbf 1_A\bigl(1-(L-b_0)^2\bigr) +\mathbf 1_B\bigl(1-(L-b_1)^2\bigr).$$ Then the optimal residual error $\mathcal R=\mathbb E[(L-\mathbb E[L\mid\mathcal O])^2]$ satisfies $$\label{eq:coarse-residual} 1-\mathcal R\ge\mathbb Eg_{b_0,b_1},\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\mathbb E[L\mid\mathcal O])\ge m+\mathbb Eg_{b_0,b_1}.$$
  - Explanation: Merging all-pass leaf values into a distinct mark preserves the pointwise coordinate bound. Predictors on the exceptional and all-pass marks give a lower bound on the optimal variance retained.
- **claim-complement-refinement-mean-advantage** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-refinement)): Let $q\ge2$, let $G=(G_1,\ldots,G_q)$ have independent standard normal coordinates, and put $S_G=\sum_iG_i$. Suppose a measurable set $A\subset\mathbb R^q$ has Gaussian-null boundary, $$p_A:=\mathbb P(G\in A)>0,\qquad r_A:=\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(S_G\mid G\in A)<1,$$ and its indicator, on every product of finite alphabets, is a finite linear combination of functions each depending on at most $q-1$ coordinates. Then there is a finite pointwise partition $\mathcal P$ with one cell $A$ of cost $q-1$ and all other cells of cost $q$ that has Gaussian-null cell boundaries and the prescribed pointwise coordinate bounds. Writing $$V=\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\mathbb E[S_G\mid\mathcal P(G)]),\qquad c=\mathbb E\ell(\mathcal P(G)),$$ we have $V>c=q-p_A$.
  - Explanation: Keeping the saving event intact and subdividing its complement into a finite rectangular grid makes the remaining prediction error smaller than the available saving. The resulting retained variance exceeds average cell cost.
- **claim-mean-cost-amplification** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-thm-mean-cost)): Let $q\ge2$, let $\mathcal P:\mathbb R^q\to\mathcal L$ be a measurable map to a finite label set, and let $\ell:\mathcal L\to\{1,\ldots,q\}$. Suppose that, on every product of finite input alphabets, each cell indicator for $l$ is a finite linear combination of functions of at most $\ell(l)$ input coordinates. Suppose also that every cell has boundary of standard Gaussian measure zero in $\mathbb R^q$. For independent standard normals $G_1,\ldots,G_q$, put $$S_G=\sum_{i=1}^qG_i,\qquad V=\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\mathbb E[S_G\mid\mathcal P(G)]),\qquad c=\mathbb E\ell(\mathcal P(G)).$$ If $V>c$, then finite observations on sign cubes have arbitrarily large ratios $v(F)/D$, where $D>0$ is a cell degree bound. The Boolean conclusion of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> follows by taking the sign of the final score, without a further averaging step. All partitions and observations are defined pointwise, including cells of zero Gaussian probability.
  - Explanation: An average cost advantage can be converted into an everywhere-valid degree bound by retaining controlled-cost output words and merging all other words. Iteration also restores the score conditions needed for a final sign readout.
- **claim-fixed-partition-lower-limit-transfer** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-mc-transfer)): Under the boundary hypothesis of Theorem <a href="#thm:mean-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mean-cost">9</a>, let $Z_n$ be centered random variables of variance one converging in distribution to a standard normal. Use $q$ independent copies to set $$S_n=\sum_{i=1}^qZ_{n,i},\qquad L_n=\mathcal P(\mathbf Z_n),\qquad V_n=\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\mathbb E[S_n\mid L_n]),\qquad c_n=\mathbb E\ell(L_n).$$ Then $$\liminf_n V_n\ge V,\qquad c_n\longrightarrow c, \qquad \mathbb E\|Z_n\|\longrightarrow\sqrt{2/\pi}.$$ No fourth-moment hypothesis is required.
  - Explanation: A fixed finite partition transfers cell probabilities, mean costs and absolute first moments under weak convergence and normalized second moments. Retained variance has a lower-limit guarantee, with no fourth-moment requirement.
- **claim-weighted-gaussian-saving-event** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-weighted-event)): For all sufficiently large finite $R$, the event $A$ just defined satisfies $$p_A:=\mathbb P(G\in A)>0,\qquad r_A:=\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(L\mid G\in A)<1.$$ Its boundary is contained in finitely many coordinate hyperplanes, and on every real input its indicator has the expansion $$\label{app:weighted-cell} \mathbf 1_A(g)= \sum_{i=1}^m\mathbf 1_{B_i}(g_0) \prod_{\substack{1\le j\le m\\j\ne i}}\chi_j(g_j) -\prod_{j=1}^m\chi_j(g_j).$$ Each product involves at most $m=q-1$ coordinates.
  - Explanation: Binning the central Gaussian coordinate and shifting leaf intervals by their bin means gives another positive-probability saving event. Its variance calculation uses the conditional bin weights, and its indicator expansion holds pointwise.

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-transfer",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transfer</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Independence gives joint weak convergence. Clipping $g$ to a bounded range gives convergence of expectations by the null-discontinuity criterion for weak convergence [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 3.10.1(vi)]. The fourth-moment bound and fixed $r$ give $\\sup_j\\mathbb E[g(\\mathbf Z_j)^2]<\\infty$, so the clipping errors in first moment tend to zero uniformly; this is the higher-moment criterion for uniform integrability [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 4.6.2]. The Gaussian error does also. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "Let $Z_j$ be centered, variance-one random variables converging in distribution to a standard normal, with $\\sup_j\\mathbb EZ_j^4<\\infty$.           "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-readout",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-readout</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Fix $C>0$ and choose $F$ with $v(F)>4C^2D$. For $M$ independent copies, define $f=\\operatorname{sign}(T_1+\\cdots+T_M)$, with sign $1$ at zero. Its degree is at most $MD$. Conditioning on the tuple of observations, $$\\sum_i\\widehat f(\\{i\\})\n =\\mathbb E[fH_{MN}]\n =\\mathbb E|T_1+\\cdots+T_M|\n =\\sqrt{Mv(F)}\\,\\mathbb E|Z_M|.$$ The central limit theorem and the uniform second-moment bound imply $\\mathbb E|Z_M|\\to\\mathbb E|G|=\\sqrt{2/\\pi}>1/2$. For a sufficiently large finite $M$, the displayed sum exceeds $C\\sqrt{MD}\\ge C\\sqrt{\\deg(f)}$. It is positive, so $f$ is nonconstant. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-amplification",
        "exact_quote": "Let $1\\le d<r$ be integers, and let $\\mathcal W$ be a measurable observation rule on $r$ real inputs. Suppose:*\n\n1.  *On any product of finite input alphabets, the indicator of each output value is a finite linear combination of functions each depending on at most $d$ input coordinates.*\n\n2.  *There is an $\\eta>0$ such that, whenever centered variance-one laws $Z_j$ converge weakly to a standard normal with uniformly bounded fourth moments, independent copies satisfy, for all sufficiently large $j$, $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}\\!\\left(\\mathbb E\\!\\left[\\sum_{i=1}^r Z_{j,i}\\,\\middle|\\,\n                    \\mathcal W(\\mathbf Z_j)\\right]\\right)\\ge d+\\eta.$$*\n\n*Then finite observations have arbitrarily large retained variance-to-cell-degree ratios, and Theorem <a href=\"#thm:main\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:main\">1</a> follows.",
        "source_id": "PM-192.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-prop-amplification",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prop-amplification</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Fix a finite observation $F$ with $v=v(F)>0$ and degree bound $D>0$. Form $r$ independent blocks of $M$ copies of $F$, and apply $\\mathcal W$ to their normalized scores from <a href=\"#eq:averaging\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:averaging\">[eq:averaging]</a>. The new observation $F'$ has finite range. Its cell degree is at most $dMD$ by the first assumption and expansion in value indicators. The second assumption applies by <a href=\"#eq:fourth\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:fourth\">[eq:fourth]</a>. For a sufficiently large finite $M$, the tower property therefore gives $$v(F')=Mv\\,\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}\\!\\left(\\mathbb E\\!\\left[\\sum_{i=1}^rZ_{M,i}\\mid F'\\right]\\right)\n \\ge Mv(d+\\eta).$$ The ratio increases by at least the fixed factor $1+\\eta/d>1$. Start with the observation of one sign, for which $v=D=1$, and iterate. Each stage is finite and has its own sufficiently large finite copy count. A finite number of stages exceeds any prescribed ratio. Apply Lemma <a href=\"#lem:readout\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:readout\">3</a>. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $1\\le d<r$ be integers, and let $\\mathcal W$ be a measurable observation rule on $r$ real inputs.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "eq:retained",
        "exact_quote": "Then $$\\label{eq:retained}\n \\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb E[L\\mid\\mathcal W])\\ge m+\\Delta_b.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-192.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-report",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-report</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Identity <a href=\"#eq:indicator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:indicator\">[eq:indicator]</a> gives the required decomposition for the $A$ report. An attained $B$ report fixes the $m$ leaf values and imposes no condition on $u$. An attained $P^c$ report fixes $u$, its selected index $i$, and the $m-1$ unselected leaves. At least one of these reported leaves fails its test; hence the reported values already force $P^c$, whatever the omitted value $z_i$ may be. Its indicator therefore depends on only the $m$ reported coordinates. Unused output labels have zero indicators. This proves the first assertion on every input, including interval endpoints.\n\nTo estimate the retained variance, predict $L$ by $b$ on $A$, by $\\sum_jz_j$ on $B$, and by $u+\\sum_{j\\ne\\iota(u)}z_j$ on $P^c$. This predictor is a function of the reported information. On $B$ its error is $u$; independence gives $\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_Bu^2]=\\mathbb P(B)$. For the last type of report, fix an index $i$ and let $E_i=P^c\\cap\\{\\iota(u)=i\\}$. This event and its reported data depend only on $u$ and the leaves other than $z_i$. Consequently $$\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_{E_i}z_i]=0,\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_{E_i}z_i^2]=\\mathbb P(E_i).$$ Summing the squared errors over the three types of report gives $$\\mathbb P(A^c)+\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_A(L-b)^2]=1-\\Delta_b.$$ Conditional expectation has no larger mean squared error [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorems 4.1.13 and 4.1.15]. Since $L$ is centered and $\\operatorname{Var}(L)=m+1$, the conditional-variance identity gives <a href=\"#eq:retained\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:retained\">[eq:retained]</a>. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every product of finite input alphabets, the rule $\\mathcal W$ has finite range, and each output indicator is a finite linear combination of functions of at most $m$ input coordinates.*\n\n\n*For independent centered variance-one inputs, whose marginal laws need not agree, and every fixed $b\\in\\mathbb R$, put $$\\Delta_b=\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_A(1-(L-b)^2)].$$ Then $$\\label{eq:retained}\n \\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb E[L\\mid\\mathcal W])\\ge m+\\Delta_b.$$",
      "explanation": "Cancellation gives the exceptional tag the same coordinate cost as the ordinary reports. A fixed predictor on that tag yields a lower bound for retained variance.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-gaussian",
        "exact_quote": "In particular, $\\mathbb P(A)>0$ and $\\operatorname{Var}(L\\mid A)<1$.",
        "source_id": "PM-192.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-gaussian",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gaussian</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The shift by one in $b$ determines where to place the intervals. Conditional on $u=t$ and $P$, the mean of $L-b$ is $1+t-\\mu_{\\iota(t)}$, so selecting an interval near $1+t$ makes this mean small. Conditional on $B$ alone, with $u$ still random, its mean is instead one. Subtracting the contribution of $B$ will turn that fixed squared mean into the positive term in $d_A$.\n\nWe let odd $m$ tend to infinity to choose the parameters, and then fix one finite value. Set $$R=\\sqrt{8\\log m},\\qquad h=m^{-3/2},\\qquad\n a_i=1-R+\\frac{2R(i-1)}{m-1},\\qquad I_i=[a_i-h,a_i+h].$$ For $|t|\\le R$, select a center $a_{\\iota(t)}$ nearest to $1+t$, resolving ties by the smaller index. For $|t|>R$, use the middle index, whose center is $1$. These choices define the rule on the whole real line. Its finitely many interval endpoints and selector cut points also give the asserted boundary property.\n\n\nWrite $\\phi(t)=(2\\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-t^2/2}$. Every $\\beta_i$ lies in $(0,1)$. Condition first on $u=t$ and put $i=\\iota(t)$. The event $P$ then has probability $p_*/\\beta_i$; it restricts the leaves $j\\ne i$ to their intervals and leaves $z_i$ unrestricted. Under this conditioning, $L-b$ has mean $1+t-\\mu_i$ and variance $1+\\sum_{j\\ne i}s_j^2$. Thus $$\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_P(1-(L-b)^2)]=-p_*J,\n \\qquad\n J=\\int_{\\mathbb R}\n \\left((1+t-\\mu_{\\iota(t)})^2+\n                   \\sum_{j\\ne\\iota(t)}s_j^2\\right)\n                 \\frac{\\phi(t)}{\\beta_{\\iota(t)}}\\,dt.$$ On $B$, with $u$ again random, $L-b$ has mean $1$ and variance $1+S$. Therefore $$\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_B(1-(L-b)^2)]=-p_*(1+S).$$ Since $\\mathbf1_A=\\mathbf1_P-\\mathbf1_B$, we obtain the exact identity $$\\label{eq:gaussian-identity}\n d_A=p_*(1+S-J).$$ The positive constant $1$ comes from the mean of $L-b$ on $B$. It remains to make the nonnegative error $J$ small. Nearest-center selection controls its central part; the fallback to the middle interval controls the tails.\n\nThe elementary interval estimates are $$|\\mu_i-a_i|\\le h,\\qquad s_i^2\\le h^2,\\qquad\n \\beta_i\\ge 2h(2\\pi)^{-1/2}\n              \\exp\\!\\left(-\\frac{(|a_i|+h)^2}{2}\\right).$$ For $|t|\\le R$, the grid spacing and the selector give $$|1+t-\\mu_{\\iota(t)}|\\le\\frac{R}{m-1}+h,\n \\qquad |a_{\\iota(t)}|+h\\le |t|+2$$ when $m$ is sufficiently large. Hence $\\phi(t)/\\beta_{\\iota(t)}\\le(e^2/2h)e^{2R}$ on this range, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n J_{\\mathrm{central}}\n &\\le\\frac{e^2R}{h}e^{2R}\n       \\left(\\left(\\frac{R}{m-1}+h\\right)^2+mh^2\\right)\\\\\n &=O\\!\\left(R(R^2+1)e^{2R}m^{-1/2}\\right)=o(1).\n\\end{aligned}$$ Here $e^{2R}=m^{o(1)}$, while $R$ is a power of $\\log m$.\n\nOn $|t|>R$, the selected interval is centered at $1$. For $h\\le1$ its probability is at least $2h\\phi(2)$, and the numerator in $J$ is $O(1+t^2)$. Integration by parts gives $$\\int_{|t|>R}(1+t^2)\\phi(t)\\,dt\n       =O((R+1)e^{-R^2/2}).$$ Consequently $$J_{\\mathrm{tail}}\n =O(h^{-1}(R+1)e^{-R^2/2})\n =O((R+1)m^{-5/2})=o(1).$$ Also $S\\le mh^2=m^{-2}\\to0$. Fix a sufficiently large finite odd $m$ so that $J<1/4$ and $S<1/4$. Identity <a href=\"#eq:gaussian-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:gaussian-identity\">[eq:gaussian-identity]</a> now proves <a href=\"#eq:deficit\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:deficit\">[eq:deficit]</a>, with $p_*>0$.\n\nFinally, write $p_A=\\mathbb P(A)$. Since $d_A>0$, we have $p_A>0$, and $$d_A=p_A\\bigl(1-\\operatorname{Var}(L\\mid A)\n                   -(\\mathbb E[L\\mid A]-b)^2\\bigr)>0.$$ This also proves $\\operatorname{Var}(L\\mid A)<1$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There are a finite odd integer $m\\ge3$, closed intervals $I_1,\\ldots,I_m$, and a selector $\\iota$ with finitely many cut points such that the following holds for independent standard normal inputs. Let $\\beta_j$ be the probability of $I_j$, and let $\\mu_j,s_j^2$ be the conditional mean and variance on $I_j$. Define $$p_* =\\prod_{j=1}^m\\beta_j,\\qquad\n S=\\sum_{j=1}^m s_j^2,\\qquad\n b=\\sum_{j=1}^m\\mu_j-1.$$ Then $p_*>0$, $S<1/4$, and $$\\label{eq:deficit}\n d_A:=\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_A(1-(L-b)^2)]>\\frac34p_*.$$ The boundary of $A$ lies in a finite union of coordinate hyperplanes. In particular, $\\mathbb P(A)>0$ and $\\operatorname{Var}(L\\mid A)<1$.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "eq:coarse-residual",
        "exact_quote": "Then the optimal residual error $\\mathcal R=\\mathbb E[(L-\\mathbb E[L\\mid\\mathcal O])^2]$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:coarse-residual}\n 1-\\mathcal R\\ge\\mathbb Eg_{b_0,b_1},\\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(\\mathbb E[L\\mid\\mathcal O])\\ge m+\\mathbb Eg_{b_0,b_1}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-192.1"

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-coarse-report",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-coarse-report</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The $X$ indicator has the pointwise expansion <a href=\"#eq:indicator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:indicator\">[eq:indicator]</a>. The $Y$ indicator depends only on the $m$ leaves. An ordinary output specifies $u$, its selected index $i$, and the $m-1$ unselected leaf values. If these data are attained, one of the reported leaves fails its interval test. They therefore force $P^c$ for every value of the omitted leaf $z_i$, so the indicator uses just the $m$ reported inputs. An inconsistent or unattained output label has zero indicator. Thus the assertion holds pointwise, including endpoints and selector ties.\n\nPredict $L$ by $b_0$ on $X$, by $b_1$ on $Y$, and by $u+\\sum_{j\\ne\\iota(u)}z_j$ on an ordinary report. For each fixed $i$, the event $E_i=P^c\\cap\\{\\iota(u)=i\\}$ and all its reported data depend only on $u$ and the leaves other than $z_i$. Independence gives $$\\mathbb E[\\mathbf 1_{E_i}z_i]=0,\\qquad\n \\mathbb E[\\mathbf 1_{E_i}z_i^2]=\\mathbb P(E_i).$$ The ordinary reports consequently contribute exactly $\\mathbb P(P^c)$ to the squared prediction error. The total error of this predictor is $$\\mathbb P(P^c)+\\mathbb E[\\mathbf 1_A(L-b_0)^2]+\\mathbb E[\\mathbf 1_B(L-b_1)^2]\n =1-\\mathbb Eg_{b_0,b_1}.$$ Conditional expectation minimizes squared error, proving the first inequality in <a href=\"#eq:coarse-residual\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:coarse-residual\">[eq:coarse-residual]</a>. The second follows because $L$ is centered with variance $m+1$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "On every product of finite input alphabets, $\\mathcal O$ has finite range, and each output indicator is a finite linear combination of functions of at most $m$ input coordinates.*\n\n\n*Let the inputs be independent, centered, and of variance one; their marginal laws need not agree. For any fixed constants $b_0,b_1\\in\\mathbb R$, define $$\\label{eq:coarse-deficit}\n g_{b_0,b_1}\n =\\mathbf 1_A\\bigl(1-(L-b_0)^2\\bigr)\n   +\\mathbf 1_B\\bigl(1-(L-b_1)^2\\bigr).$$ Then the optimal residual error $\\mathcal R=\\mathbb E[(L-\\mathbb E[L\\mid\\mathcal O])^2]$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:coarse-residual}\n 1-\\mathcal R\\ge\\mathbb Eg_{b_0,b_1},\\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(\\mathbb E[L\\mid\\mathcal O])\\ge m+\\mathbb Eg_{b_0,b_1}.$$",
      "explanation": "Merging all-pass leaf values into a distinct mark preserves the pointwise coordinate bound. Predictors on the exceptional and all-pass marks give a lower bound on the optimal variance retained.",

        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-refinement",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-refinement</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Partition $[-M,M)$ into finitely many half-open intervals of length at most $h$, and add the two tail intervals. Approximate a real coordinate by the midpoint of its bounded interval and by zero on the tails. For a standard normal coordinate, the expected squared error is at most $$h^2+\\mathbb E[G_1^2\\mathbf 1_{\\{|G_1|\\ge M\\}}].$$ Let $\\widetilde S_G$ be the sum of these coordinate approximations. It is constant on each box of the resulting finite rectangular grid. Since $(\\sum_i a_i)^2\\le q\\sum_i a_i^2$, choosing finite $M$ large enough and then $h>0$ small enough ensures $$\\mathbb E(S_G-\\widetilde S_G)^2<p_A(1-r_A).$$\n\nGive $A$ one label and give every intersection of $A^c$ with a grid box its own label. Retain this partition pointwise, even on cells of zero Gaussian probability. Every new cell has boundary contained in the boundary of $A$ together with the finitely many grid hyperplanes, and hence has Gaussian-null boundary. Give $A$ cost $q-1$ and every other cell cost $q$. The special-cell algebraic requirement is assumed; the others are automatic for functions of $q$ inputs. Conditional means minimize squared error on every cell [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 4.1.15], so $$\\mathbb E\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(S_G\\mid\\mathcal P(G))\n \\le p_A r_A+\n       \\mathbb E[\\mathbf 1_{A^c}(S_G-\\widetilde S_G)^2]<p_A.$$ Since $\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(S_G)=q$, it follows that $V>q-p_A=\\mathbb E\\ell(\\mathcal P(G))=c$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $q\\ge2$, let $G=(G_1,\\ldots,G_q)$ have independent standard normal coordinates, and put $S_G=\\sum_iG_i$. Suppose a measurable set $A\\subset\\mathbb R^q$ has Gaussian-null boundary, $$p_A:=\\mathbb P(G\\in A)>0,\\qquad\n r_A:=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(S_G\\mid G\\in A)<1,$$ and its indicator, on every product of finite alphabets, is a finite linear combination of functions each depending on at most $q-1$ coordinates. Then there is a finite pointwise partition $\\mathcal P$ with one cell $A$ of cost $q-1$ and all other cells of cost $q$ that has Gaussian-null cell boundaries and the prescribed pointwise coordinate bounds. Writing $$V=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(\\mathbb E[S_G\\mid\\mathcal P(G)]),\\qquad\n c=\\mathbb E\\ell(\\mathcal P(G)),$$ we have $V>c=q-p_A$.",
      "explanation": "Keeping the saving event intact and subdividing its complement into a finite rectangular grid makes the remaining prediction error smaller than the available saving. The resulting retained variance exceeds average cell cost.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-mean-cost",
        "exact_quote": "If $V>c$, then finite observations on sign cubes have arbitrarily large ratios $v(F)/D$, where $D>0$ is a cell degree bound. The Boolean conclusion of Theorem <a href=\"#thm:main\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:main\">1</a> follows by taking the sign of the final score, without a further averaging step.",
        "source_id": "PM-192.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-mc-transfer-2",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mc-transfer</summary>\n\n\n*Proof of Theorem <a href=\"#thm:mean-cost\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:mean-cost\">9</a>.* The partition and costs are fixed throughout the proof. Choose $$0<\\delta<(V-c)/2,\n \\qquad 1<\\lambda<\\frac{V-\\delta}{c+\\delta}.$$ For any centered, variance-one law $Z$, use independent copies to define $$S_Z=\\sum_{i=1}^q Z_i,\\qquad L=\\mathcal P(\\mathbf Z),\\qquad\n V_Z=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(\\mathbb E[S_Z\\mid L]),\\qquad c_Z=\\mathbb E\\ell(L).$$ For a finite observation $F$ with $v=v(F)>0$, write $Z=T_F/\\sqrt v$. We will increase $v/D$ while maintaining $$\\label{eq:invariant}\n V_Z>V-\\delta,\\qquad c_Z<c+\\delta/2,\\qquad \\mathbb E|Z|>1/2.$$ Lemma <a href=\"#lem:mc-transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:mc-transfer\">10</a> shows that all three inequalities hold eventually along every sequence of centered, variance-one laws converging to a standard normal.\n\nTo initialize, observe the bit sum itself: $F=H_N$, with score $T_F=H_N$ and $v=D=N$. The central limit theorem for independent uniform signs [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 3.4.1] supplies a finite $N$ for which <a href=\"#eq:invariant\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:invariant\">[eq:invariant]</a> holds. The initial ratio is $v/D=1$.\n\n\nNow hold one such $F$ fixed. Apply $\\mathcal P$ to the standardized scores of $q$ independent copies of $F$, obtaining the group label $L$. The algebraic assumption and the degree calculus give $$\\deg(\\mathbf 1_{\\{L=l\\}})\\le D\\ell(l).$$ The tower property identifies the group score and its variance as $$\\label{eq:group}\n T_L=\\sqrt v\\,\\mathbb E[S_Z\\mid L],\\qquad\n \\tau^2:=v(L)=vV_Z>0.$$ Thus this group has a variance advantage relative to its mean cost. We next obtain a common degree bound without losing that advantage.\n\n\nTake $K$ independent copies of $L$. Retain a label word $(l_1,\\ldots,l_K)$ when $\\sum_{b=1}^K\\ell(l_b)\\le K(c+\\delta)$. Let $F'_K$ report each retained word exactly and report one failure label for every other word. Every retained-word indicator is a product with degree at most $$\\label{eq:degree}\n D'_K=DK(c+\\delta).$$ The failure indicator is one minus the finite sum of all retained-word indicators, so it satisfies the same degree bound on every input. The real upper bound $D'_K$ need not be rounded to an integer. Since the independent costs have mean $c_Z<c+\\delta/2$, the law of large numbers [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 2.2.3] gives a failure probability $\\varepsilon_K\\to0$.\n\n\nWrite $U_K=\\sum_{b=1}^KT_{L_b}$ and $T'_K=T_{F'_K}$. The tower property again gives $T'_K=\\mathbb E[U_K\\mid F'_K]$. On a retained word, $U_K$ is known exactly. On failure, its conditional mean is at least as good a predictor as zero [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 4.1.15]. Consequently $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathbb E(U_K-T'_K)^2\n &\\le\\mathbb E[U_K^2\\mathbf 1_{\\{\\mathrm{failure}\\}}]\\notag\\\\\n &\\le(\\mathbb EU_K^4)^{1/2}\\varepsilon_K^{1/2}\n   =o(K\\tau^2). \\label{eq:loss}\n\\end{aligned}$$ For the last equality, $T_L$ has finite range, mean zero, and positive variance, and independence gives $$\\mathbb EU_K^4=K\\mathbb ET_L^4+3K(K-1)\\tau^4=O(K^2\\tau^4).$$ The implicit constant can depend on the current observation, which has been fixed before $K$ tends to infinity.\n\nThe conditional-expectation projection identity [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 4.1.15 and its proof] now yields $$v(F'_K)=K\\tau^2-\\mathbb E(U_K-T'_K)^2\\sim K\\tau^2.$$ In particular, this variance is positive for all sufficiently large $K$. The central limit theorem [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 3.4.1] gives $U_K/(\\sqrt K\\tau)\\Rightarrow N(0,1)$. By <a href=\"#eq:loss\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:loss\">[eq:loss]</a>, replacing $U_K$ by $T'_K$ changes the normalized variable by a term tending to zero in $L^2$. Moreover, $\\sqrt{v(F'_K)}/(\\sqrt K\\tau)\\to1$. The addition and rescaling forms of Slutsky’s theorem [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Exercises 3.2.13–3.2.14] therefore give $$\\frac{T'_K}{\\sqrt{v(F'_K)}}\\Rightarrow N(0,1).$$ These new standardized scores are centered and have variance one. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:mc-transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:mc-transfer\">10</a> restores all three inequalities in <a href=\"#eq:invariant\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:invariant\">[eq:invariant]</a> for sufficiently large $K$. At the same time, <a href=\"#eq:group\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:group\">[eq:group]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:degree\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:degree\">[eq:degree]</a> give $$\\frac{v(F'_K)}{D'_K}\\longrightarrow\n \\frac vD\\frac{V_Z}{c+\\delta}>\\lambda\\frac vD.$$ One sufficiently large finite $K$ therefore restores every maintained condition and increases the ratio by a factor greater than $\\lambda$.\n\nFor any prescribed $C>0$, choose a positive integer $k$ with $\\lambda^k>4C^2$. Perform $k$ such steps, choosing a finite batch size separately at each step. The final observation remains finite and satisfies $$v(F)/D>4C^2,\\qquad \\mathbb E|T_F|>\\tfrac12\\sqrt{v(F)}.$$ Let $N$ now denote the total number of bits underlying the final observation. Its sign readout $f=\\operatorname{sign}(T_F)$, with sign $1$ at zero, has degree at most $D$. Conditioning the bit sum on $F$ gives the signed identity $$\\sum_i\\widehat f(\\{i\\})=\\mathbb E[fH_N]=\\mathbb E|T_F|\n >\\tfrac12\\sqrt{v(F)}>C\\sqrt D\\ge C\\sqrt{\\deg(f)}.$$ The positive signed correlation excludes constant $f$. Arbitrarily large target ratios follow from the same finite iteration. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $q\\ge2$, let $\\mathcal P:\\mathbb R^q\\to\\mathcal L$ be a measurable map to a finite label set, and let $\\ell:\\mathcal L\\to\\{1,\\ldots,q\\}$. Suppose that, on every product of finite input alphabets, each cell indicator for $l$ is a finite linear combination of functions of at most $\\ell(l)$ input coordinates. Suppose also that every cell has boundary of standard Gaussian measure zero in $\\mathbb R^q$. For independent standard normals $G_1,\\ldots,G_q$, put $$S_G=\\sum_{i=1}^qG_i,\\qquad\n V=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(\\mathbb E[S_G\\mid\\mathcal P(G)]),\\qquad\n c=\\mathbb E\\ell(\\mathcal P(G)).$$ If $V>c$, then finite observations on sign cubes have arbitrarily large ratios $v(F)/D$, where $D>0$ is a cell degree bound. The Boolean conclusion of Theorem <a href=\"#thm:main\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:main\">1</a> follows by taking the sign of the final score, without a further averaging step. All partitions and observations are defined pointwise, including cells of zero Gaussian probability.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-mc-transfer",
        "exact_quote": "Then $$\\liminf_n V_n\\ge V,\\qquad c_n\\longrightarrow c,\n \\qquad \\mathbb E|Z_n|\\longrightarrow\\sqrt{2/\\pi}.$$ No fourth-moment hypothesis is required.",
        "source_id": "PM-192.1"

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-mc-transfer",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mc-transfer</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Independence gives joint weak convergence of the input tuples. The Gaussian-null boundaries imply convergence of all cell probabilities [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 3.10.1(v)]. For a fixed cell $E$, clip the sum continuously to $[-t,t]$. The clipped sum times $\\mathbf 1_E$ is bounded and has Gaussian-null discontinuities, so its expectations converge [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 3.10.1(vi)]. Since $S_n$ is centered and has variance $q$, $$\\mathbb E\\bigl[|S_n|\\mathbf 1_{\\{|S_n|>t\\}}\\bigr]\\le q/t.$$ Removing the clipping therefore proves $\\mathbb E[S_n\\mathbf 1_{\\{\\mathbf Z_n\\in E\\}}]\\to\n\\mathbb E[S_G\\mathbf 1_{\\{G\\in E\\}}]$.\n\nFor each $n$, the centered conditional mean satisfies $$V_n=\\sum_{l:\\mathbb P(L_n=l)>0}\n \\frac{\\mathbb E[S_n\\mathbf 1_{\\{L_n=l\\}}]^2}{\\mathbb P(L_n=l)}.$$ Every term for a cell of positive Gaussian probability converges to the corresponding Gaussian term. All remaining terms are nonnegative. This proves the lower limit inequality; it does not require deleting the cells with zero limiting probability. The cost convergence follows from the finitely many convergent cell probabilities. Finally, weak convergence and the bound $\\mathbb E[|Z_n|\\mathbf 1_{\\{|Z_n|>t\\}}]\\le1/t$ give convergence of the absolute first moments. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Under the boundary hypothesis of Theorem <a href=\"#thm:mean-cost\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:mean-cost\">9</a>, let $Z_n$ be centered random variables of variance one converging in distribution to a standard normal. Use $q$ independent copies to set $$S_n=\\sum_{i=1}^qZ_{n,i},\\qquad L_n=\\mathcal P(\\mathbf Z_n),\\qquad\n V_n=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(\\mathbb E[S_n\\mid L_n]),\\qquad c_n=\\mathbb E\\ell(L_n).$$ Then $$\\liminf_n V_n\\ge V,\\qquad c_n\\longrightarrow c,\n \\qquad \\mathbb E|Z_n|\\longrightarrow\\sqrt{2/\\pi}.$$ No fourth-moment hypothesis is required.",
      "explanation": "A fixed finite partition transfers cell probabilities, mean costs and absolute first moments under weak convergence and normalized second moments. Retained variance has a lower-limit guarantee, with no fourth-moment requirement.",

      "id": "claim-fixed-partition-lower-limit-transfer",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-weighted-event",
        "exact_quote": "For all sufficiently large finite $R$, the event $A$ just defined satisfies $$p_A:=\\mathbb P(G\\in A)>0,\\qquad r_A:=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(L\\mid G\\in A)<1.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-192.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-weighted-event",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-weighted-event</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Exactly one bin contains $g_0$. The first term in <a href=\"#app:weighted-cell\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"app:weighted-cell\">[app:weighted-cell]</a> tests all unselected leaves; subtracting the all-leaves-pass indicator requires the selected leaf to fail. This proves the identity pointwise, including all endpoints. The boundary assertion follows from the finite collections of bin and leaf-interval endpoints. It remains to prove the variance saving. We let $R\\to\\infty$ for this calculation and fix a finite value only after the estimates.\n\n\n###### Conditional moments.\n\n\nWrite $\\phi$ for the standard normal density. Symmetry of the two tails gives $v_m=0$; for $i<m$, the conditional mean lies in the closure of $B_i$, so $|v_i|\\le R$ and $u_i\\le\\Delta^2$. Integration by parts gives $$\\int_R^\\infty t^2\\phi(t)\\,dt\n =R\\phi(R)+\\int_R^\\infty\\phi(t)\\,dt,\n \\qquad\n \\int_R^\\infty\\phi(t)\\,dt\\le\\frac{\\phi(R)}R.$$ Thus $$\\label{app:weighted-bin-moments}\n \\pi_m u_m=O(Re^{-R^2/2}),\\qquad\n \\sum_i\\pi_i v_i=0,\\qquad\n \\sum_i\\pi_i v_i^2=1-\\sum_i\\pi_i u_i=1-o(1),$$ where the last equality follows from $\\sum_i\\pi_i u_i\\le\\Delta^2+O(Re^{-R^2/2})=o(1)$.\n\n\nFor the leaf intervals, write $$\\alpha_j=\\mathbb P(G_j\\in I_j),\\qquad\n t_j=\\mathbb E[G_j\\mid G_j\\in I_j],\\qquad\n r_j=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(G_j\\mid G_j\\in I_j).$$ The bounded normal density and the interval lengths imply, uniformly in $j$ for sufficiently large $R$, $$\\label{app:weighted-rare-moments}\n 0<\\alpha_j\\le Ch<\\tfrac12,\\qquad\n |t_j-(v_j+1)|\\le h/2,\\qquad 0\\le r_j\\le h^2.$$ Constants in this section are absolute unless stated otherwise. Let $d_j,b_j$ be the conditional mean and variance of $G_j$ on $I_j^c$. Using the unconditional mean zero and variance one gives $$\\label{app:weighted-complement}\n d_j=-\\frac{\\alpha_jt_j}{1-\\alpha_j},\\qquad\n b_j=\\frac{1-\\alpha_jr_j}{1-\\alpha_j}\n       -\\frac{\\alpha_jt_j^2}{(1-\\alpha_j)^2}.$$ Indeed the complementary second moment is $(1-\\alpha_j(r_j+t_j^2))/(1-\\alpha_j)$, from which we subtract $d_j^2$.\n\n\n###### The mixture identity.\n\n\n\nThe event $A$ changes the distribution of the selected bin. Put $$w_i=\\pi_i\\frac{1-\\alpha_i}{\\alpha_i},\\qquad\n W=\\sum_iw_i,\\qquad \\omega_i=\\frac{w_i}{W}.$$ Independence gives $$\\label{app:weighted-probability}\n p_A=W\\prod_{j=1}^m\\alpha_j>0,\n \\qquad \\mathbb P(G_0\\in B_i\\mid G\\in A)=\\omega_i.$$ Here $W$ is finite and positive for every finite $R$. Within $A\\cap\\{G_0\\in B_i\\}$ the coordinates remain independent under their respective bin, interval, and complementary-interval restrictions. The conditional mean and variance of $L$ are therefore $$\\sum_{j=1}^mt_j-1+e_i,\n \\qquad u_i+\\sum_jr_j+b_i-r_i,\n \\qquad e_i=v_i+1-\\frac{t_i}{1-\\alpha_i}.$$ The small mean error satisfies $$\\label{app:weighted-mean-error}\n |e_i|\\le h/2+\\frac{\\alpha_i|t_i|}{1-\\alpha_i}\n          =O(h(R+2)).$$ Applying the variance decomposition with the conditional weights $\\omega_i$, not the original weights $\\pi_i$, gives the exact identity $$\\begin{aligned}\n W\\bigl(1-\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(L\\mid G\\in A)\\bigr)\n &=\\sum_iw_i(1-b_i+r_i)-\\sum_iw_iu_i\\notag\\\\\n &\\quad-W\\sum_jr_j-W\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}_{i\\sim\\omega}(e_i).\n \\label{app:weighted-variance-identity}\n\\end{aligned}$$ We next show that the first sum has lower limit at least one and that each of the three subtracted terms tends to zero. This will give the strict conditional variance bound despite the possibly large total weight $W$.\n\nThe schedule in <a href=\"#app:weighted-schedule\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"app:weighted-schedule\">[app:weighted-schedule]</a> makes the bin scale $\\Delta^2/h$, the aggregate leaf scale $mh$, and the tail scale $e^{-R^2/2}/h$ tend to zero even after multiplication by $e^{3R}$ and any fixed power of $R$. The leaf intervals are narrow enough to control their total variance, but wide enough that inverse interval probabilities do not overwhelm the bin and tail estimates below.\n\n\n###### The positive contribution.\n\n\nSubstituting <a href=\"#app:weighted-complement\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"app:weighted-complement\">[app:weighted-complement]</a> yields $$\\label{app:weighted-positive-term}\n w_i(1-b_i+r_i)\n =\\pi_i\\left(-1+\\frac{r_i}{\\alpha_i}\n                    +\\frac{t_i^2}{1-\\alpha_i}\\right)\n \\ge\\pi_i(t_i^2-1).$$ Since $t_i=v_i+1+O(h)$ uniformly, we have $$\\sum_i\\pi_i(t_i^2-1)\n =\\sum_i\\pi_i v_i^2+2\\sum_i\\pi_i v_i+O(h(R+2))\n =1+o(1)$$ by <a href=\"#app:weighted-bin-moments\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"app:weighted-bin-moments\">[app:weighted-bin-moments]</a>. The shift of the leaf intervals by one is important: it produces the term $t_i^2-1$, whose average tends to one.\n\n\n###### The three error terms.\n\nFor $i<m$, the fact that $v_i$ belongs to the closure of $B_i$ gives the density-ratio bound $$\\begin{aligned}\n w_i\\le\\frac{\\pi_i}{\\alpha_i}\n &\\le\\frac{\\Delta}{h}\n       \\frac{\\phi(\\max\\{0,|v_i|-\\Delta\\})}\n            {\\phi(|v_i|+1+h)}\\notag\\\\\n &\\le\\frac{\\Delta}{h}e^{3R}\n \\label{app:weighted-density-ratio}\n\\end{aligned}$$ for all sufficiently large $R$. For the final inequality, put $x=|v_i|\\le R$. The logarithm of the density ratio is at most $$\\frac{(x+1+h)^2-\\max\\{0,x-\\Delta\\}^2}{2}\n \\le(1+h+\\Delta)x+\\frac{(1+h)^2}{2}\\le3R.$$ The middle inequality follows by expanding when $x\\ge\\Delta$; when $x<\\Delta$, use $x^2/2\\le\\Delta x$. For the tail bin, $v_m=0$, so $\\alpha_m\\ge h\\phi(1+h)$ and $w_m\\le C\\pi_m/h$. Summing and using $(m-1)\\Delta=2R$ gives $$\\label{app:weighted-total-weight}\n W\\le\\frac{2Re^{3R}+C}{h}.$$\n\nFirst, $u_i\\le\\Delta^2$ on interior bins and $\\pi_mu_m=O(Re^{-R^2/2})$ on the tail bin. Since $\\Delta^2\\le4R^2e^{-R^2/2}$, we obtain $$\\sum_iw_iu_i\n \\le W\\Delta^2+C\\pi_mu_m/h\n =O(R^3e^{3R-R^2/8})+O(Re^{-R^2/8})=o(1).$$ Second, $\\sum_jr_j\\le mh^2$ and $mh=O(e^{-R^2/8})$, so $$W\\sum_jr_j\\le Wmh^2\n =O((Re^{3R}+1)e^{-R^2/8})=o(1).$$ Third, <a href=\"#app:weighted-mean-error\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"app:weighted-mean-error\">[app:weighted-mean-error]</a> gives $$W\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}_{i\\sim\\omega}(e_i)\n \\le W\\,O(h^2(R+2)^2)\n =O((Re^{3R}+1)(R+2)^2e^{-3R^2/8})=o(1).$$ Each negative quadratic exponent dominates the linear exponent and polynomial factors. Combining these estimates with <a href=\"#app:weighted-variance-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"app:weighted-variance-identity\">[app:weighted-variance-identity]</a> and <a href=\"#app:weighted-positive-term\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"app:weighted-positive-term\">[app:weighted-positive-term]</a> shows that $$W\\bigl(1-\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(L\\mid G\\in A)\\bigr)\\ge1+o(1)>0$$ for all sufficiently large $R$. Since $W>0$, this proves the lemma. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For all sufficiently large finite $R$, the event $A$ just defined satisfies $$p_A:=\\mathbb P(G\\in A)>0,\\qquad r_A:=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(L\\mid G\\in A)<1.$$ Its boundary is contained in finitely many coordinate hyperplanes, and on every real input its indicator has the expansion $$\\label{app:weighted-cell}\n \\mathbf 1_A(g)=\n \\sum_{i=1}^m\\mathbf 1_{B_i}(g_0)\n       \\prod_{\\substack{1\\le j\\le m\\\\j\\ne i}}\\chi_j(g_j)\n       -\\prod_{j=1}^m\\chi_j(g_j).$$ Each product involves at most $m=q-1$ coordinates.",
      "explanation": "Binning the central Gaussian coordinate and shifting leaf intervals by their bin means gives another positive-probability saving event. Its variance calculation uses the conditional bin weights, and its indicator expansion holds pointwise.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-weighted-event",
          "exact_quote": "For all sufficiently large finite $R$,",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "claim-weighted-gaussian-saving-event",
      "kind": "construction",
      "quantifiers": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-weighted-event",
          "exact_quote": "For all sufficiently large finite $R$,",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-weighted-event",
          "exact_quote": "on every real input",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-lem-weighted-event"
    }
  ],
  "generation_status": "COMPLETE",
  "pm_id": "PM-192.1",
  "schema": "pm.claims.v2"
}

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Fourier coefficient and real multilinear degree** ([source](#pm-h-2)): For a real-valued function $f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\mathbb R$, with $X$ uniform on the sign cube, write $$\widehat f(S)=\mathbb E\!\left[f(X)\prod_{i\in S}X_i\right], \qquad \deg(f)=\max\{\|S\|:\widehat f(S)\ne0\}.$$
- **Majority benchmark** ([source](#pm-h-3)): A sharper proposal in the same problem list uses the majority benchmark $$B_d=\mathbb E\|X_1+\cdots+X_d\|\le\sqrt d.$$
- **Coordinate influence and total influence** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Let $\operatorname{Inf}_i(f)$ be the probability that flipping coordinate $i$ changes its value, and let $I(f)=\sum_i\operatorname{Inf}_i(f)$ be the total influence.
- **Observation score and retained variance** ([source](#pm-h-4)): If $H_N=X_1+\cdots+X_N$, the information that $F$ retains about this sum is measured by $$T_F=\mathbb E[H_N\mid F],\qquad v(F)=\mathbb ET_F^2.$$
- **Standardized score** ([source](#pm-h-4)): When $v(F)>0$, its standardized score is $T_F/\sqrt{v(F)}$.
- **Cell degree bound** ([source](#pm-h-4)): We call a positive real number $D$ a *cell degree bound* if every output indicator $\mathbf 1_{\{F=a\}}$ has degree at most $D$.
- **Exceptional reporting event** ([source](#eq:indicator)): Define $$B=\{z_j\in I_j\text{ for every }j\},\qquad P=\{z_j\in I_j\text{ for every }j\ne\iota(u)\},\qquad A=P\setminus B.$$
- **Refined tagged report** ([source](#eq:indicator)): Define the tagged observation $\mathcal W$ by $$\begin{array}{c\|l} \text{Event}&\text{Information reported}\\ \hline A&\text{the tag }A\text{ only}\\ B&(B,z_1,\ldots,z_m)\\ P^c&(P^c,u,\iota(u),(z_j)_{j\ne\iota(u)}). \end{array}$$
- **Coarse report** ([source](#subsec:coarse-mark)): Define the *coarse report* $\mathcal O$ by $$\begin{array}{c\|l} \text{Event}&\text{Information reported}\\ \hline A&\text{the mark }X\\ B&\text{the mark }Y\\ P^c&(O,u,\iota(u),(z_j)_{j\ne\iota(u)}). \end{array}$$
- **Label coordinate cost** ([source](#sec:refinement-first)): Give label $l$ an integer cost $\ell(l)\in\{1,\ldots,q\}$.
- **Mean-cost iteration invariant** ([source](#eq:invariant)): We will increase $v/D$ while maintaining $$\label{eq:invariant} V_Z>V-\delta,\qquad c_Z<c+\delta/2,\qquad \mathbb E\|Z\|>1/2.$$

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-ambient-singleton-bound | Signed singleton sum | ≤ | $\sqrt n$ | The ambient dimension of the Boolean function. |
| q-surviving-linear-degree-bound | Absolute singleton sum | ≤ | $\deg(f)$ | Every Boolean function; total influence is an intermediate upper bound. |
| q-averaged-score-fourth-moment | Fourth moment of the normalized independent score sum | = | $\frac{\mathbb ET_F^4}{Mv^2}+3\frac{M-1}{M}$ | Uniformity is in the averaging count for the current observation. |
| q-readout-variance-threshold | Retained variance chosen for Boolean readout | > | $4C^2D$ | The observation is chosen for the prescribed positive factor. |
| q-fixed-cost-ratio-multiplier | Guaranteed retained variance-to-degree ratio multiplier | = | $1+\eta/d$ | The guaranteed factor is fixed; each stage chooses its own sufficiently large finite copy count. |
| q-refined-retained-variance | Variance retained by the refined report | ≥ | $m+\Delta_b$ | Fixed reporting intervals, selector and exceptional-event predictor. |
| q-gaussian-deficit-margin | Gaussian exceptional-event predictor deficit | > | $\frac34p_*$ | A sufficiently large finite odd leaf count is fixed. |
| q-gaussian-event-conditional-variance | Conditional coordinate-sum variance on the exceptional event | < | $1$ | The event has positive probability. |
| q-coarse-gaussian-predictor-gain | Expected deficit of the coarse Gaussian predictor | > | $\frac34p_*$ | The exceptional predictor and all-pass predictor use the fixed Gaussian interval means. |
| q-refinement-average-cost | Expected label cost after complement refinement | = | $q-p_A$ | The saving event costs less than every complement cell. |
| q-typical-word-common-degree-bound | Common cell degree bound after retained-word merging | = | $DK(c+\delta)$ | Every retained word and the merged failure cell obey the same real degree bound. |
| q-partition-variance-lower-limit | Lower limit of retained variance under finite-partition transfer | ≥ | $V$ | Only a lower-limit guarantee is asserted for retained variance. |
| q-weighted-event-conditional-variance | Conditional sum variance for the weighted-mixture event | < | $1$ | The truncation parameter is fixed after the asymptotic saving has been established. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-transfer-fixed-predictor-errors** [claim](#stmt-lem-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-transfer): Use joint weak convergence for clipped functions and uniform integrability to remove clipping. -> Convergence of the fixed predictor-error expectation.
- **step-readout-large-score-ratio** [claim](#stmt-lem-readout) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-readout): Average independent scores and take the sign; identify singleton correlation through conditional expectation. -> A nonconstant Boolean function violating the prescribed square-root degree factor.
- **step-amplify-fixed-coordinate-cost** [claim](#stmt-prop-amplification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-amplification): Compose the rule with normalized independent score blocks and iterate the fixed ratio improvement. -> Arbitrarily large retained variance-to-cell-degree ratios and the Boolean conclusion.
- **step-refined-report-cancellation-and-error** [claim](#stmt-lem-report) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-report): Decompose the exceptional indicator by cancellation and compare conditional expectation with a report-measurable predictor. -> The pointwise coordinate-cost bound and the retained-variance lower bound.
- **step-choose-gaussian-saving-parameters** [claim](#stmt-lem-gaussian) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gaussian): Use shifted interval centers, nearest-center selection and a central fallback in the tails; bound the exact Gaussian error identity. -> Fixed finite Gaussian parameters with a positive predictor deficit and conditional variance saving.
- **step-transfer-gain-and-prove-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-9): Transfer the fixed Gaussian deficit to normalized finite score sums, apply amplification and finish with Boolean readout. -> The signed counterexamples and unbounded absolute singleton-degree ratio.
- **step-coarse-report-cancellation-and-error** [claim](#stmt-lem-coarse-report) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-coarse-report): Merge all-pass outputs, retain distinct tags, and evaluate the errors of fixed predictors on both special cells. -> The same coordinate-cost bound and a coarse-report retained-variance lower bound.
- **step-refine-complement-grid** [claim](#stmt-lem-refinement) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-refinement): Keep the saving event as a cell and refine its complement using a finite rectangular grid with controlled squared approximation error. -> A finite partition with retained variance strictly exceeding its average coordinate cost.
- **step-transfer-fixed-partition-moments** [claim](#stmt-lem-mc-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-mc-transfer): Transfer cell probabilities and clipped restricted first moments, then retain the nonnegative terms associated with zero limiting cell probabilities. -> A retained-variance lower-limit bound, mean-cost convergence and absolute first-moment convergence.
- **step-amplify-mean-cost-advantage** [claim](#stmt-thm-mean-cost) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-mc-transfer-2): Retain controlled-cost label words, merge the remaining words, bound variance loss and restore the normalized-score invariant before each iteration. -> Arbitrarily large retained variance-to-degree ratios and a final Boolean sign readout without additional averaging.
- **step-weighted-mixture-saving-event** [claim](#stmt-lem-weighted-event) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-weighted-event): Condition on selected bins using the event-reweighted probabilities, derive the exact variance identity and show the subtracted error terms vanish. -> An alternative pointwise saving event with positive probability, Gaussian-null boundary and conditional variance below the unobserved-input variance.
- **step-apply-mean-cost-to-saving-events** [claim](#stmt-thm-mean-cost) / [proof](#app:weighted-transfer): Fix the event parameters, refine the complement, and apply the fixed-partition transfer and mean-cost amplification criterion. -> Another route to the main signed Boolean conclusion.
- **step-small-width-coarse-transfer** [claim](#stmt-prop-amplification) / [proof](#app:small-width-transfer): Fix a small-width Gaussian schedule and transfer its law-dependent predictor coefficients through restricted selector-cell moments. -> An alternative verification of the fixed-cost amplification hypotheses.

          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-report</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Identity <a href=\"#eq:indicator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:indicator\">[eq:indicator]</a> gives the required decomposition for the $A$ report. An attained $B$ report fixes the $m$ leaf values and imposes no condition on $u$. An attained $P^c$ report fixes $u$, its selected index $i$, and the $m-1$ unselected leaves. At least one of these reported leaves fails its test; hence the reported values already force $P^c$, whatever the omitted value $z_i$ may be. Its indicator therefore depends on only the $m$ reported coordinates. Unused output labels have zero indicators. This proves the first assertion on every input, including interval endpoints.\n\nTo estimate the retained variance, predict $L$ by $b$ on $A$, by $\\sum_jz_j$ on $B$, and by $u+\\sum_{j\\ne\\iota(u)}z_j$ on $P^c$. This predictor is a function of the reported information. On $B$ its error is $u$; independence gives $\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_Bu^2]=\\mathbb P(B)$. For the last type of report, fix an index $i$ and let $E_i=P^c\\cap\\{\\iota(u)=i\\}$. This event and its reported data depend only on $u$ and the leaves other than $z_i$. Consequently $$\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_{E_i}z_i]=0,\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_{E_i}z_i^2]=\\mathbb P(E_i).$$ Summing the squared errors over the three types of report gives $$\\mathbb P(A^c)+\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_A(L-b)^2]=1-\\Delta_b.$$ Conditional expectation has no larger mean squared error [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorems 4.1.13 and 4.1.15]. Since $L$ is centered and $\\operatorname{Var}(L)=m+1$, the conditional-variance identity gives <a href=\"#eq:retained\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:retained\">[eq:retained]</a>. ◻",

    },

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-gaussian",
      "depends_on": [
        "step-refined-report-cancellation-and-error"
      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-gaussian",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gaussian</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The shift by one in $b$ determines where to place the intervals. Conditional on $u=t$ and $P$, the mean of $L-b$ is $1+t-\\mu_{\\iota(t)}$, so selecting an interval near $1+t$ makes this mean small. Conditional on $B$ alone, with $u$ still random, its mean is instead one. Subtracting the contribution of $B$ will turn that fixed squared mean into the positive term in $d_A$.\n\nWe let odd $m$ tend to infinity to choose the parameters, and then fix one finite value. Set $$R=\\sqrt{8\\log m},\\qquad h=m^{-3/2},\\qquad\n a_i=1-R+\\frac{2R(i-1)}{m-1},\\qquad I_i=[a_i-h,a_i+h].$$ For $|t|\\le R$, select a center $a_{\\iota(t)}$ nearest to $1+t$, resolving ties by the smaller index. For $|t|>R$, use the middle index, whose center is $1$. These choices define the rule on the whole real line. Its finitely many interval endpoints and selector cut points also give the asserted boundary property.\n\n\nWrite $\\phi(t)=(2\\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-t^2/2}$. Every $\\beta_i$ lies in $(0,1)$. Condition first on $u=t$ and put $i=\\iota(t)$. The event $P$ then has probability $p_*/\\beta_i$; it restricts the leaves $j\\ne i$ to their intervals and leaves $z_i$ unrestricted. Under this conditioning, $L-b$ has mean $1+t-\\mu_i$ and variance $1+\\sum_{j\\ne i}s_j^2$. Thus $$\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_P(1-(L-b)^2)]=-p_*J,\n \\qquad\n J=\\int_{\\mathbb R}\n \\left((1+t-\\mu_{\\iota(t)})^2+\n                   \\sum_{j\\ne\\iota(t)}s_j^2\\right)\n                 \\frac{\\phi(t)}{\\beta_{\\iota(t)}}\\,dt.$$ On $B$, with $u$ again random, $L-b$ has mean $1$ and variance $1+S$. Therefore $$\\mathbb E[\\mathbf1_B(1-(L-b)^2)]=-p_*(1+S).$$ Since $\\mathbf1_A=\\mathbf1_P-\\mathbf1_B$, we obtain the exact identity $$\\label{eq:gaussian-identity}\n d_A=p_*(1+S-J).$$ The positive constant $1$ comes from the mean of $L-b$ on $B$. It remains to make the nonnegative error $J$ small. Nearest-center selection controls its central part; the fallback to the middle interval controls the tails.\n\nThe elementary interval estimates are $$|\\mu_i-a_i|\\le h,\\qquad s_i^2\\le h^2,\\qquad\n \\beta_i\\ge 2h(2\\pi)^{-1/2}\n              \\exp\\!\\left(-\\frac{(|a_i|+h)^2}{2}\\right).$$ For $|t|\\le R$, the grid spacing and the selector give $$|1+t-\\mu_{\\iota(t)}|\\le\\frac{R}{m-1}+h,\n \\qquad |a_{\\iota(t)}|+h\\le |t|+2$$ when $m$ is sufficiently large. Hence $\\phi(t)/\\beta_{\\iota(t)}\\le(e^2/2h)e^{2R}$ on this range, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n J_{\\mathrm{central}}\n &\\le\\frac{e^2R}{h}e^{2R}\n       \\left(\\left(\\frac{R}{m-1}+h\\right)^2+mh^2\\right)\\\\\n &=O\\!\\left(R(R^2+1)e^{2R}m^{-1/2}\\right)=o(1).\n\\end{aligned}$$ Here $e^{2R}=m^{o(1)}$, while $R$ is a power of $\\log m$.\n\nOn $|t|>R$, the selected interval is centered at $1$. For $h\\le1$ its probability is at least $2h\\phi(2)$, and the numerator in $J$ is $O(1+t^2)$. Integration by parts gives $$\\int_{|t|>R}(1+t^2)\\phi(t)\\,dt\n       =O((R+1)e^{-R^2/2}).$$ Consequently $$J_{\\mathrm{tail}}\n =O(h^{-1}(R+1)e^{-R^2/2})\n =O((R+1)m^{-5/2})=o(1).$$ Also $S\\le mh^2=m^{-2}\\to0$. Fix a sufficiently large finite odd $m$ so that $J<1/4$ and $S<1/4$. Identity <a href=\"#eq:gaussian-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:gaussian-identity\">[eq:gaussian-identity]</a> now proves <a href=\"#eq:deficit\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:deficit\">[eq:deficit]</a>, with $p_*>0$.\n\nFinally, write $p_A=\\mathbb P(A)$. Since $d_A>0$, we have $p_A>0$, and $$d_A=p_A\\bigl(1-\\operatorname{Var}(L\\mid A)\n                   -(\\mathbb E[L\\mid A]-b)^2\\bigr)>0.$$ This also proves $\\operatorname{Var}(L\\mid A)<1$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "pm-h-9",
          "exact_quote": "#### Transfer to finite inputs and amplification\n\nFix all the Gaussian choices from Lemma <a href=\"#lem:gaussian\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:gaussian\">6</a>, including $m$, the intervals, the selector, $b$, and $p_*>0$. Set $\\eta=p_*/2$. If centered variance-one laws $Z_n$ converge weakly to a standard normal and have uniformly bounded fourth moments, apply Lemma <a href=\"#lem:transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:transfer\">2</a> to the fixed function $$g(u,z_1,\\ldots,z_m)=\\mathbf1_A(1-(L-b)^2).$$ It has quadratic growth and discontinuities only on the finitely many hyperplanes from Lemma <a href=\"#lem:gaussian\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:gaussian\">6</a>; these have Gaussian measure zero. For independent copies of $Z_n$, therefore, $\\mathbb Eg\\to d_A>3p_*/4$, and eventually $\\mathbb Eg>\\eta$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:report\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:report\">5</a> then gives $$\\operatorname{Var}\\!\\left(\n   \\mathbb E\\!\\left[\\sum_{i=1}^{m+1} Z_{n,i}\n                   \\,\\middle|\\,\\mathcal W(\\mathbf Z_n)\\right]\\right)\n >m+\\eta.$$ Together with the pointwise output-indicator decomposition in that lemma, this verifies both hypotheses of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:amplification\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:amplification\">4</a> with $(r,d,\\eta)=(m+1,m,p_*/2)$.\n\nThe Gaussian parameters and $\\eta$ remain fixed throughout the iteration. Proposition <a href=\"#prop:amplification\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:amplification\">4</a> therefore increases the ratio of retained variance to cell degree bound by at least $1+\\eta/m>1$ at each stage, using a sufficiently large finite copy count chosen for the current observation. Starting from one revealed sign, finitely many stages make this ratio exceed $4C^2$ for any prescribed $C>0$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:readout\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:readout\">3</a> then supplies a nonconstant Boolean function with signed singleton sum greater than $C\\sqrt{\\deg(f)}$, proving Theorem <a href=\"#thm:main\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:main\">1</a>.",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-coarse-report",
      "depends_on": [

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-coarse-report",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-coarse-report</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The $X$ indicator has the pointwise expansion <a href=\"#eq:indicator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:indicator\">[eq:indicator]</a>. The $Y$ indicator depends only on the $m$ leaves. An ordinary output specifies $u$, its selected index $i$, and the $m-1$ unselected leaf values. If these data are attained, one of the reported leaves fails its interval test. They therefore force $P^c$ for every value of the omitted leaf $z_i$, so the indicator uses just the $m$ reported inputs. An inconsistent or unattained output label has zero indicator. Thus the assertion holds pointwise, including endpoints and selector ties.\n\nPredict $L$ by $b_0$ on $X$, by $b_1$ on $Y$, and by $u+\\sum_{j\\ne\\iota(u)}z_j$ on an ordinary report. For each fixed $i$, the event $E_i=P^c\\cap\\{\\iota(u)=i\\}$ and all its reported data depend only on $u$ and the leaves other than $z_i$. Independence gives $$\\mathbb E[\\mathbf 1_{E_i}z_i]=0,\\qquad\n \\mathbb E[\\mathbf 1_{E_i}z_i^2]=\\mathbb P(E_i).$$ The ordinary reports consequently contribute exactly $\\mathbb P(P^c)$ to the squared prediction error. The total error of this predictor is $$\\mathbb P(P^c)+\\mathbb E[\\mathbf 1_A(L-b_0)^2]+\\mathbb E[\\mathbf 1_B(L-b_1)^2]\n =1-\\mathbb Eg_{b_0,b_1}.$$ Conditional expectation minimizes squared error, proving the first inequality in <a href=\"#eq:coarse-residual\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:coarse-residual\">[eq:coarse-residual]</a>. The second follows because $L$ is centered with variance $m+1$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],

      ],
      "method": "Merge all-pass outputs, retain distinct tags, and evaluate the errors of fixed predictors on both special cells.",
      "output": "The same coordinate-cost bound and a coarse-report retained-variance lower bound.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-coarse-report"

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-refinement",
      "depends_on": [],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-refinement",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-refinement</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Partition $[-M,M)$ into finitely many half-open intervals of length at most $h$, and add the two tail intervals. Approximate a real coordinate by the midpoint of its bounded interval and by zero on the tails. For a standard normal coordinate, the expected squared error is at most $$h^2+\\mathbb E[G_1^2\\mathbf 1_{\\{|G_1|\\ge M\\}}].$$ Let $\\widetilde S_G$ be the sum of these coordinate approximations. It is constant on each box of the resulting finite rectangular grid. Since $(\\sum_i a_i)^2\\le q\\sum_i a_i^2$, choosing finite $M$ large enough and then $h>0$ small enough ensures $$\\mathbb E(S_G-\\widetilde S_G)^2<p_A(1-r_A).$$\n\nGive $A$ one label and give every intersection of $A^c$ with a grid box its own label. Retain this partition pointwise, even on cells of zero Gaussian probability. Every new cell has boundary contained in the boundary of $A$ together with the finitely many grid hyperplanes, and hence has Gaussian-null boundary. Give $A$ cost $q-1$ and every other cell cost $q$. The special-cell algebraic requirement is assumed; the others are automatic for functions of $q$ inputs. Conditional means minimize squared error on every cell [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 4.1.15], so $$\\mathbb E\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(S_G\\mid\\mathcal P(G))\n \\le p_A r_A+\n       \\mathbb E[\\mathbf 1_{A^c}(S_G-\\widetilde S_G)^2]<p_A.$$ Since $\\mathop{\\mathrm{Var}}(S_G)=q$, it follows that $V>q-p_A=\\mathbb E\\ell(\\mathcal P(G))=c$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-refine-complement-grid",
      "inputs": [
        "Positive-probability event with conditional variance saving",
        "Gaussian-null boundary",
        "Pointwise special-cell coordinate saving"
      ],
      "method": "Keep the saving event as a cell and refine its complement using a finite rectangular grid with controlled squared approximation error.",
      "output": "A finite partition with retained variance strictly exceeding its average coordinate cost.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-refinement"
    },

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-mc-transfer",
      "depends_on": [],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-mc-transfer",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mc-transfer</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Independence gives joint weak convergence of the input tuples. The Gaussian-null boundaries imply convergence of all cell probabilities [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 3.10.1(v)]. For a fixed cell $E$, clip the sum continuously to $[-t,t]$. The clipped sum times $\\mathbf 1_E$ is bounded and has Gaussian-null discontinuities, so its expectations converge [[Durrett](#ref-Durrett), Theorem 3.10.1(vi)]. Since $S_n$ is centered and has variance $q$, $$\\mathbb E\\bigl[|S_n|\\mathbf 1_{\\{|S_n|>t\\}}\\bigr]\\le q/t.$$ Removing the clipping therefore proves $\\mathbb E[S_n\\mathbf 1_{\\{\\mathbf Z_n\\in E\\}}]\\to\n\\mathbb E[S_G\\mathbf 1_{\\{G\\in E\\}}]$.\n\nFor each $n$, the centered conditional mean satisfies $$V_n=\\sum_{l:\\mathbb P(L_n=l)>0}\n \\frac{\\mathbb E[S_n\\mathbf 1_{\\{L_n=l\\}}]^2}{\\mathbb P(L_n=l)}.$$ Every term for a cell of positive Gaussian probability converges to the corresponding Gaussian term. All remaining terms are nonnegative. This proves the lower limit inequality; it does not require deleting the cells with zero limiting probability. The cost convergence follows from the finitely many convergent cell probabilities. Finally, weak convergence and the bound $\\mathbb E[|Z_n|\\mathbf 1_{\\{|Z_n|>t\\}}]\\le1/t$ give convergence of the absolute first moments. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-192.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-transfer-fixed-partition-moments",
      "inputs": [
        "Fixed finite partition with Gaussian-null boundaries",
        "Independent centered variance-one inputs converging weakly"
      ],
      "method": "Transfer cell probabilities and clipped restricted first moments, then retain the nonnegative terms associated with zero limiting cell probabilities.",
      "output": "A retained-variance lower-limit bound, mean-cost convergence and absolute first-moment convergence.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-mc-transfer"
